People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle things, but that’s when I started feeling like this whole change in me really began.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and low mood seem mainly tied to your daughter leaving for the military?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to put it all on that, but that’s really when I noticed the change. Since she left Georgia for the military, it’s been about two weeks of me feeling this way. I guess that lines up, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, some. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but things I’d usually enjoy just don’t pull me in right now. I used to mess with my tomato plants, sand wood out at my bench, watch the Falcons, stay after church and talk with folks… lately I just kind of look at all that like it belongs to somebody else, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been since this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve been sleeping, I guess, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it or anything. It may be a little off, but I don’t want to say more than I know. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know that I would’ve called it depressed, maybe just “in a low spell” or not quite myself. Looking back, it’s been happening off and on for maybe about a year now… stretches where I’d feel down for days, sometimes longer. But this one since my daughter left, this has felt the hardest, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, was there ever a time when feeling down lasted for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I don’t want to overstate it, but looking back those low stretches have been showing up off and on for about a year. Some of them may have run a couple weeks, maybe longer. This time after my daughter left is the one that’s hit me the hardest, though.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, did you ever have a stretch of feeling down nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… looking back, those low stretches have been coming around for about a year now. I maybe didn’t pay close attention to whether it was every single day, but there were times it felt pretty steady for a couple weeks or more. This one after my daughter left has definitely hit me the hardest, though.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, during those earlier low stretches, did you ever have thoughts that you’d be better off dead or not wake up?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud. It wasn’t like I had some big plan or anything, but I did keep having these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. That’s actually part of why I came in… it scared me enough that I told my supervisor. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Between those earlier low periods and this current one, was there ever a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I’d like to say I bounced back completely, but I don’t know if it was for a full two months. There were times I was doing better and kind of getting through things normally, but whether that was my “usual self” for that long… I’m not sure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Between those earlier low periods and this current one, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that for sure. There were stretches where I was doing better and trying to act normal, you know, keeping up with things. But a full two months of feeling really like myself… I’m not sure. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I’m here because I’ve been having some thoughts I don’t really like to admit. Nothing I’m proud of. I keep thinking maybe my family would be better off if I just… didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough that I finally told my supervisor.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been about two weeks now, since my daughter left Georgia for the military. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle things, but that’s when I started feeling like this whole change in me really began.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and low mood seem mainly tied to your daughter leaving for the military?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to put it all on that, but that’s really when I noticed the change. Since she left Georgia for the military, it’s been about two weeks of me feeling this way. I guess that lines up, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, some. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but things I’d usually enjoy just don’t pull me in right now. I used to mess with my tomato plants, sand wood out at my bench, watch the Falcons, stay after church and talk with folks… lately I just kind of look at all that like it belongs to somebody else, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been since this started?\n\nPatient: I’m not real sure how to answer that. I mean… I’ve been sleeping, I guess, but I haven’t really been keeping track of it or anything. It may be a little off, but I don’t want to say more than I know. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this for days or weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t know that I would’ve called it depressed, maybe just “in a low spell” or not quite myself. Looking back, it’s been happening off and on for maybe about a year now… stretches where I’d feel down for days, sometimes longer. But this one since my daughter left, this has felt the hardest, if that answers what you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, was there ever a time when feeling down lasted for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, probably. I don’t want to overstate it, but looking back those low stretches have been showing up off and on for about a year. Some of them may have run a couple weeks, maybe longer. This time after my daughter left is the one that’s hit me the hardest, though.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, did you ever have a stretch of feeling down nearly every day for two weeks or longer?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… looking back, those low stretches have been coming around for about a year now. I maybe didn’t pay close attention to whether it was every single day, but there were times it felt pretty steady for a couple weeks or more. This one after my daughter left has definitely hit me the hardest, though.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, during those earlier low stretches, did you ever have thoughts that you’d be better off dead or not wake up?\n\nPatient: I… yeah. I mean, I don’t like saying that out loud. It wasn’t like I had some big plan or anything, but I did keep having these thoughts that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn’t wake up. That’s actually part of why I came in… it scared me enough that I told my supervisor. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Between those earlier low periods and this current one, was there ever a time when you felt back to your usual self for at least two months?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I’d like to say I bounced back completely, but I don’t know if it was for a full two months. There were times I was doing better and kind of getting through things normally, but whether that was my “usual self” for that long… I’m not sure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Between those earlier low periods and this current one, was there ever a stretch of at least two months when you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: I honestly don’t know if I can say that for sure. There were stretches where I was doing better and trying to act normal, you know, keeping up with things. But a full two months of feeling really like myself… I’m not sure. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer.\n\nDoctor: If your daughter hadn’t left for the military, do you think you would still be feeling this depressed right now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, her leaving definitely hit me hard, so part of me wants to say maybe I’d be doing better. But I can’t honestly say I’d be completely fine if she hadn’t gone. I’ve had low times before, so… I’m not sure how to separate it. Is that a fair answer?\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, has how depressed you feel seemed stronger than you would expect from that change alone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, it feels heavy, but I also don’t know what’s “normal” for something like that, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It just… affected me a lot, but whether it’s more than you’d expect, I can’t really say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
